Aurora DSQL Connector for Go pgx
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Go wraps pgx with automatic IAM authentication. The connector 
handles token generation, SSL conﬁguration, and connection management so you can focus on 
your application logic.
About the connector
Aurora DSQL requires IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens that existing Go 
PostgreSQL drivers do not natively support. The Aurora DSQL Connector for Go adds an 
authentication layer on top of the pgx driver that handles IAM token generation, allowing you to 
connect to Aurora DSQL without changing your existing pgx workﬂows.
What is Aurora DSQL Authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM Authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens
• Token Generation: The connector generates authentication tokens using AWS credentials, and 
these tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Go is designed to understand these requirements and 
automatically generate IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.
Go connector 295
=====PAGE 308=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Beneﬁts of the Aurora DSQL Connector for Go
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Go allows you to continue using your existing pgx workﬂows while 
enabling IAM authentication through:
• Automatic Token Generation: The connector generates IAM tokens automatically for each 
connection
• Connection Pooling: Built-in support for pgxpool with automatic token generation per 
connection
• Flexible Conﬁguration: Support for full endpoints or cluster IDs with region auto-detection
• AWS Credentials Support: Supports AWS proﬁles and custom credentials providers
Key features
Automatic Token Management
The connector generates IAM tokens automatically for each new connection using pre-resolved 
credentials.
Connection Pooling
Connection pooling via pgxpool with automatic token generation per connection.
Flexible Host Conﬁguration
Supports both full cluster endpoints and cluster IDs with automatic region detection.
SSL Security
SSL is always enabled with verify-full mode and direct TLS negotiation.
Prerequisites
• Go 1.24 or later
• AWS credentials conﬁgured
• An Aurora DSQL cluster
The connector uses the AWS SDK for Go v2 default credential chain, which resolves credentials in 
the following order:
Go connector 296
=====PAGE 309=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
1. Environment variables (AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY)
2. Shared credentials ﬁle (~/.aws/credentials)
3. Shared conﬁg ﬁle (~/.aws/conﬁg)
4. IAM role for Amazon EC2/ECS/Lambda
Installation
Install the connector using Go modules:
go get github.com/awslabs/aurora-dsql-connectors/go/pgx/dsql
Quick start
The following example shows how to create a connection pool and execute a query:
package main
import ( 
    "context" 
    "log" 
    "github.com/awslabs/aurora-dsql-connectors/go/pgx/dsql"
)
func main() { 
    ctx := context.Background() 
    // Create a connection pool 
    pool, err := dsql.NewPool(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
        Host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
    }) 
    if err != nil { 
        log.Fatal(err) 
    } 
    defer pool.Close() 
    // Execute a query 
    var greeting string 
    err = pool.QueryRow(ctx, "SELECT 'Hello, DSQL!'").Scan(&greeting) 
    if err != nil { 
Go connector 297
=====PAGE 310=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
        log.Fatal(err) 
    } 
    log.Println(greeting)
}

Connection string format
The connector supports PostgreSQL and DSQL connection string formats:
Go connector 298
=====PAGE 311=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
postgres://[user@]host[:port]/[database][?param=value&...]
dsql://[user@]host[:port]/[database][?param=value&...]
Supported query parameters:
• region - AWS region
• profile - AWS proﬁle name
• tokenDurationSecs - Token validity duration in seconds
Examples:
// Full endpoint (region auto-detected)
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, "postgres://admin@cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres")
// Using dsql:// scheme (also supported)
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, "dsql://admin@cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres")
// With explicit region
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, "postgres://admin@cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/mydb?
region=us-east-1")
// With AWS profile
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, "postgres://admin@cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres?
profile=dev")
Advanced usage
Host conﬁguration
The connector supports two host formats:
Full endpoint (region auto-detected):
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
    Host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws",
})
Cluster ID (region required):
pool, _ := dsql.NewPool(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
Go connector 299
=====PAGE 312=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
    Host:   "your-cluster-id", 
    Region: "us-east-1",
})
Pool conﬁguration tuning
Conﬁgure the connection pool for your workload:
pool, err := dsql.NewPool(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
    Host:              "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
    MaxConns:          20, 
    MinConns:          5, 
    MaxConnLifetime:   time.Hour, 
    MaxConnIdleTime:   30 * time.Minute, 
    HealthCheckPeriod: time.Minute,
})
Single connection usage
For simple scripts or when connection pooling is not needed:
conn, err := dsql.Connect(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
    Host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws",
})
if err != nil { 
    log.Fatal(err)
}
defer conn.Close(ctx)
// Use the connection
rows, err := conn.Query(ctx, "SELECT * FROM users")
Using AWS proﬁles
Specify an AWS proﬁle for credentials:
pool, err := dsql.NewPool(ctx, dsql.Config{ 
    Host:    "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
    Profile: "production",
})
Go connector 300
=====PAGE 313=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
OCC retry
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). When two transactions modify the same 
data, the ﬁrst to commit wins and the second receives an OCC error.
The occretry package provides helpers for automatic retry with exponential backoﬀ and jitter. 
Install it with:
go get github.com/awslabs/aurora-dsql-connectors/go/pgx/occretry
Use WithRetry for transactional writes:
err := occretry.WithRetry(ctx, pool, occretry.DefaultConfig(), func(tx pgx.Tx) error { 
    _, err := tx.Exec(ctx, "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - $1 WHERE id = $2", 
 100, fromID) 
    if err != nil { 
        return err 
    } 
    _, err = tx.Exec(ctx, "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + $1 WHERE id = $2", 
 100, toID) 
    return err
})
For DDL or single statements, use ExecWithRetry:
err := occretry.ExecWithRetry(ctx, pool, occretry.DefaultConfig(), 
    "CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id UUID PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT)")
Important
WithRetry manages BEGIN/COMMIT/ROLLBACK internally. Your callback receives a 
transaction and should contain only database operations and be safe to retry.
Examples
For more comprehensive examples and use cases, refer to the Aurora DSQL Connector for Go 
examples.
Go connector 301
=====PAGE 314=====

Aurora DSQL Connectors for Node.js
The Aurora DSQL Connector for node-postgres and the Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js are 
authentication plugins that extend the functionality of the node-postgres and Postgres.js clients to 
enable applications to authenticate with Aurora DSQL using IAM credentials.
Aurora DSQL Connector for node-postgres
The Aurora DSQL Connector for node-postgres is a Node.js connector built on node-postgres that 
integrates IAM Authentication for connecting JavaScript/TypeScript applications to Amazon Aurora 
DSQL clusters.
The Aurora DSQL Connector is designed as an authentication plugin that extends the functionality 
of the node-postgres’ Client and Pool to enable applications to authenticate with Amazon Aurora 
DSQL using IAM credentials.
About the Connector
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a cloud-native distributed database with PostgreSQL compatibility. While 
it requires IAM authentication and time-bound tokens, traditional Node.js database drivers lack this 
built-in support.
The Aurora DSQL Connector for node-postgres bridges this gap by implementing an authentication 
middleware that works seamlessly with node-postgres. This approach allows developers to 
maintain their existing node-postgres code while gaining secure IAM-based access to Aurora DSQL 
clusters through automated token management.
What is Aurora DSQL Authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
Node.js connectors 302
=====PAGE 315=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• IAM Authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens
• Token Generation: Authentication tokens are generated using AWS credentials and have 
conﬁgurable lifetimes
The Aurora DSQL Connector for node-postgres is designed to understand these requirements and 
automatically generate IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.
Features
• Automatic IAM Authentication - Handles DSQL token generation and refresh
• Built on node-postgres - Leverages the popular PostgreSQL client for Node.js
• Seamless Integration - Works with existing node-postgres connection patterns
• Region Auto-Discovery - Extracts AWS region from DSQL cluster hostname
• Full TypeScript Support - Provides full type safety
• AWS Credentials Support - Supports various AWS credential providers (default, proﬁle-based, 
custom)
• Connection Pooling Compatibility - Works seamlessly with built-in connection pooling
Example Application
There is an included sample application in example that shows how to use Aurora DSQL Connector 
for node-postgres. To run the included example please refer to the example README.
Quick start guide
Requirements
• Node.js 20+
• Access to an Aurora DSQL cluster
• Set up appropriate IAM permissions to allow your application to connect to Aurora DSQL.
• AWS credentials conﬁgured (via AWS CLI, environment variables, or IAM roles)
Installation
npm install @aws/aurora-dsql-node-postgres-connector

Client Connection
import { AuroraDSQLClient } from "@aws/aurora-dsql-node-postgres-connector";
const client = new AuroraDSQLClient({ 
  host: "<CLUSTER_ENDPOINT>", 
  user: "admin",
});
await client.connect();

import { AuroraDSQLPool } from "@aws/aurora-dsql-node-postgres-connector";

  host: "<CLUSTER_ENDPOINT>", 
  user: "admin", 
  max: 3, 
  idleTimeoutMillis: 60000,

  user: "admin", 
  customCredentialsProvider: fromNodeProviderChain(), // Optionally provide custom 
 credentials provider

const result = await client.query("SELECT NOW()");

Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js is a Node.js connector built on Postgres.js that 
integrates IAM Authentication for connecting JavaScript applications to Amazon Aurora DSQL 
clusters.
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js is designed as an authentication plugin that extends 
the functionality of the Postgres.js client to enable applications to authenticate with Amazon 
Aurora DSQL using IAM credentials. The connector does not connect directly to the database, but 
provides seamless IAM authentication on top of the underlying Postgres.js driver.
About the Connector
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a distributed SQL database service that provides high availability 
and scalability for PostgreSQL-compatible applications. Aurora DSQL requires IAM-based 
authentication with time-limited tokens that existing Node.js drivers do not natively support.
The idea behind the Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js is to add an authentication layer on top 
of the Postgres.js client that handles IAM token generation, allowing users to connect to Aurora 
DSQL without changing their existing Postgres.js workﬂows.
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js works with most versions of Postgres.js. Users provide 
their own version by installing Postgres.js directly.
What is Aurora DSQL Authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM Authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens
• Token Generation: Authentication tokens are generated using AWS credentials and have 
conﬁgurable lifetimes
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Postgres.js is designed to understand these requirements and 
automatically generate IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.
Features
• Automatic IAM Authentication - Handles DSQL token generation and refresh
Node.js connectors 306
=====PAGE 319=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• Built on Postgres.js - Leverages the fast PostgreSQL client for Node.js
• Seamless Integration - Works with existing Postgres.js connection patterns
• Region Auto-Discovery - Extracts AWS region from DSQL cluster hostname
• Full TypeScript Support - Provides full type safety
• AWS Credentials Support - Supports various AWS credential providers (default, proﬁle-based, 
custom)
• Connection Pooling Compatibility - Works seamlessly with Postgres.js’ built-in connection

authentication for connecting Ruby applications to Amazon Aurora DSQL clusters.
The connector handles token generation, SSL conﬁguration, and connection pooling so you can 
focus on your application logic.

Amazon Aurora DSQL requires IAM authentication with time-limited tokens that existing Ruby 
PostgreSQL drivers do not natively support. The Aurora DSQL Connector for Ruby adds an 
authentication layer on top of the pg gem that handles IAM token generation, allowing you to 
connect to Aurora DSQL without changing your existing pg workﬂows.
What is Aurora DSQL authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM Authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens
• Token Generation: The connector generates authentication tokens using AWS credentials, and 
these tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes
The Aurora DSQL Connector for Ruby understands these requirements and automatically generates 
IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.

• OCC retry - Opt-in optimistic concurrency control retry with exponential backoﬀ

Ruby connector 311
=====PAGE 324=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Usage
Pool connection
require "aurora_dsql_pg"
# Create a connection pool with OCC retry enabled
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
  occ_max_retries: 3
)
# Read
pool.with do |conn| 
  result = conn.exec("SELECT 'Hello, DSQL!'") 
  puts result[0]["?column?"]
end
# Write — you must wrap writes in a transaction
pool.with do |conn| 
  conn.transaction do 
    conn.exec_params("INSERT INTO users (id, name) VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), $1)", 
 ["Alice"]) 
  end
end
pool.shutdown
Single connection
For simple scripts or when connection pooling is not needed:
conn = AuroraDsql::Pg.connect(host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws")
conn.exec("SELECT 1")
conn.close
Advanced usage
Host conﬁguration
The connector supports both full cluster endpoints (region auto-detected) and cluster IDs (region 
required):
Ruby connector 312
=====PAGE 325=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
# Full endpoint (region auto-detected)
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws"
)
# Cluster ID (region required)
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  host: "your-cluster-id", 
  region: "us-east-1"
)
AWS proﬁles
Specify an AWS proﬁle for credentials:
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
  profile: "production"
)
Connection string format
The connector supports PostgreSQL connection string formats:
postgres://[user@]host[:port]/[database][?param=value&...]
postgresql://[user@]host[:port]/[database][?param=value&...]
Supported query parameters: region, profile, tokenDurationSecs.
# Full endpoint with profile
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  "postgres://admin@cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres?profile=dev"
)
OCC retry
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). When two transactions modify the same 
data, the ﬁrst to commit wins and the second receives an OCC error.
OCC retry is opt-in. Set occ_max_retries when creating the pool to enable automatic retry with 
exponential backoﬀ and jitter on pool.with:
Ruby connector 313
=====PAGE 326=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
pool = AuroraDsql::Pg.create_pool( 
  host: "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
  occ_max_retries: 3
)
pool.with do |conn| 
  conn.transaction do 
    conn.exec_params("UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - $1 WHERE id = $2", [100, 
 from_id]) 
    conn.exec_params("UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + $1 WHERE id = $2", [100, 
 to_id]) 
  end
end
Warning
pool.with does NOT automatically wrap your block in a transaction. You must call
conn.transaction yourself for write operations. On OCC conﬂict the connector re-
executes the entire block, so it should contain only database operations and be safe to 
retry.
To skip retry on individual calls, pass retry_occ: false:
pool.with(retry_occ: false) do |conn| 
  conn.exec("SELECT 1")
end

region String (auto-detected) AWS region; required if host is a 
cluster ID
user String "admin" Database user

What is Aurora DSQL authentication?

• Token Generation: The connector generates authentication tokens using AWS credentials, and 
these tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes
The Aurora DSQL Connector for PHP understands these requirements and automatically generates 
IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.

);

// Multi-statement transactions are retried via transaction()

    $conn->exec("UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1");

Usage
Pool connection
using Amazon.AuroraDsql.Npgsql;
// Create a connection pool
await using var ds = await AuroraDsql.CreateDataSourceAsync(new DsqlConfig
{ 
    Host = "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws", 
    OccMaxRetries = 3
});
// Read
await using (var conn = await ds.OpenConnectionAsync())
{ 
    await using var cmd = conn.CreateCommand(); 
    cmd.CommandText = "SELECT 'Hello, DSQL!'"; 
    var greeting = await cmd.ExecuteScalarAsync(); 
    Console.WriteLine(greeting);
}
// Transactional write with OCC retry
await ds.WithTransactionRetryAsync(async conn =>
{ 
    await using var cmd = conn.CreateCommand(); 
    cmd.CommandText = "INSERT INTO users (id, name) VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), @name)"; 
    cmd.Parameters.AddWithValue("name", "Alice"); 
    await cmd.ExecuteNonQueryAsync();
});
.NET connector 322
=====PAGE 335=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Single connection
For simple scripts or when you do not need connection pooling:
await using var conn = await AuroraDsql.ConnectAsync(new DsqlConfig
{ 
    Host = "your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws"
});
await using var cmd = conn.CreateCommand("SELECT 1");
await cmd.ExecuteScalarAsync();
OCC retry
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). When two transactions modify the same 
data, the ﬁrst to commit wins and the second receives an OCC error.
OCC retry is opt-in. Set OccMaxRetries in the conﬁg to enable automatic retry with exponential 
backoﬀ and jitter. Use WithTransactionRetryAsync for transactional writes:
await ds.WithTransactionRetryAsync(async conn =>
{ 
    await using var cmd = conn.CreateCommand(); 
    cmd.CommandText = "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = @from"; 
    cmd.Parameters.AddWithValue("from", fromId); 
    await cmd.ExecuteNonQueryAsync(); 
    cmd.CommandText = "UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = @to"; 
    cmd.Parameters.Clear(); 
    cmd.Parameters.AddWithValue("to", toId); 
    await cmd.ExecuteNonQueryAsync();
});
For DDL or single statements, use ExecWithRetryAsync:
await ds.ExecWithRetryAsync("CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id UUID PRIMARY KEY, 
 name TEXT)");

        "postgres://admin@your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres" 
    ).await?; 
    // Read

Rust connector 327
=====PAGE 340=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
    // Write — you must wrap writes in a transaction 
    let mut tx = pool.begin().await?; 
    sqlx::query("INSERT INTO users (id, name) VALUES (gen_random_uuid(), $1)") 
        .bind("Alice")

Single connection

use sqlx::Row;
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> anyhow::Result<()> {

        "postgres://admin@your-cluster.dsql.us-east-1.on.aws/postgres" 
    ).await?; 
    let row = sqlx::query("SELECT 1 as value") 
        .fetch_one(&mut conn) 
        .await?; 
    let value: i32 = row.get("value"); 
    println!("Result: {}", value); 
    Ok(())
}

the token duration, create a new connection.

OCC retry
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). When two transactions modify the same 
data, the ﬁrst to commit wins and the second receives an OCC error.
OCC retry is opt-in. Enable the occ feature and use retry_on_occ to enable automatic retry with 
exponential backoﬀ and jitter:
use aurora_dsql_sqlx_connector::{retry_on_occ, OCCRetryConfig};
let config = OCCRetryConfig::default(); // max_attempts: 3, exponential backoff
retry_on_occ(&config, || async { 
    let mut tx = pool.begin().await?; 
    sqlx::query("UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = $1") 
        .bind(account_id) 
        .execute(&mut *tx) 
        .await?; 
    tx.commit().await?; 
    Ok(())
}).await?;

Rust connector 331
=====PAGE 344=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Token generation
• Connection pools: A background task refreshes the token at 80% of the token duration. Call
pool.close().await to stop the refresh task and release pool resources.
• Single connections: The connector generates a fresh token at connection time.
• Token generation is a local SigV4 presigning operation with negligible cost.
Admin vs regular users
• Users named "admin" automatically use admin authentication tokens
• All other users use regular authentication tokens
• The connector generates tokens dynamically for each connection
Adapters and dialects for Aurora DSQL
Adapters and dialects for Amazon Aurora DSQL provide IAM authentication and improved Aurora 
DSQL compatibility for speciﬁc object-relational mapping (ORM) frameworks. Use an adapter or 
dialect when you work with a supported ORM framework.
For a list of all adapters and dialects, including Drizzle ORM, see Aurora DSQL adapters and 
dialects.
For more information about adapter and dialect availability, see the Aurora DSQL ORMs repository
on the GitHub website.
Aurora DSQL dialect for Hibernate
The Amazon Aurora DSQL dialect for Hibernate integrates Hibernate object-relational mapping 
(ORM) with Aurora DSQL. With the dialect, your Java applications can use the ORM capabilities 
of Hibernate while taking advantage of the distributed architecture and high availability of 
Aurora DSQL. For Java Database Connectivity (JDBC), the dialect uses the PostgreSQL JDBC Driver 
internally, and the Aurora DSQL JDBC Connector for IAM authentication. For more information, 
see the Aurora DSQL Dialect for Hibernate and the Aurora DSQL JDBC Connector on the GitHub 
website, and the PostgreSQL JDBC Driver website.
The Aurora DSQL Dialect for Hibernate extends and overrides Hibernate's SQL generation to match 
Aurora DSQL's semantics, including asynchronous index creation, optimistic concurrency control 
Adapters and dialects 332
=====PAGE 345=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
(OCC), universally unique identiﬁer (UUID) primary keys, and OCC-based foreign key enforcement. 
The dialect layers above the JDBC driver and generates correct SQL for Aurora DSQL.
About the dialect
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a serverless, distributed relational database service optimized 
for transactional workloads. Aurora DSQL has speciﬁc requirements, including IAM-based 
authentication, asynchronous index creation, optimistic concurrency control, and OCC-based 
foreign key enforcement, where foreign key conﬂicts raise a serialization error that your application 
can retry.
With the Aurora DSQL Dialect for Hibernate, you can use the standard Hibernate APIs without 
manually accounting for the behavioral diﬀerences of Aurora DSQL. The dialect addresses these 
requirements by customizing the Hibernate SQL generation layer.
What is Aurora DSQL authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens.
• Token generation: The connector generates authentication tokens from your AWS credentials. 
Tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes. Aurora DSQL enforces a maximum connection duration of 
3,600 seconds (one hour) regardless of token lifetime; recycle connections before they reach this 
limit.
The Aurora DSQL JDBC Connector generates tokens automatically, so you can authenticate to 
Aurora DSQL with the Hibernate dialect without changing your existing Hibernate workﬂows.
Features
The dialect provides the following features:
• Aurora DSQL-compatible SQL generation – Generates SQL that conforms to Aurora DSQL 
semantics.
• Async index creation – Creates all indexes with CREATE INDEX ASYNC and CREATE UNIQUE 
INDEX ASYNC.
• Foreign key support – DDL export includes FOREIGN KEY constraints with OCC-based 
enforcement. Foreign key (FK) conﬂicts raise a serialization error that your application can retry. 
Hibernate dialect 333
=====PAGE 346=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
For the foreign key options that Aurora DSQL supports, see Working with foreign key constraints 
in Aurora DSQL.
• Optimistic concurrency control – Supports the NONE (OCC) and PESSIMISTIC_WRITE lock 
modes.
• UUID primary keys – Registers a UUID JDBC type for correct binding. Use
@ColumnDefault("gen_random_uuid()") to give the ID column a server-side default.
• Sequence and identity support – Generates correct DDL with the mandatory CACHE 65536
parameter.
• Connection pooling – Works with HikariCP. To keep connections in the pool after errors that 
don't aﬀect the connection, add your own DsqlExceptionOverride class, as shown in Keeping 
healthy connections with DsqlExceptionOverride.
Quick start guide
Requirements
Before you use the dialect, make sure that you have the following:
• Java 17 or higher
• Hibernate ORM version 7.2 or higher (Spring Boot 4.x for Spring Boot projects)
• Access to an Amazon Aurora DSQL cluster
• Set up appropriate IAM permissions to allow your application to connect to Aurora DSQL.
• AWS credentials conﬁgured (via AWS CLI, environment variables, or IAM roles)
• PostgreSQL JDBC driver version 42.x or higher
Installation
Add the aurora-dsql-hibernate-dialect dependency to your project.
Maven
Add the following dependency to your pom.xml ﬁle:
<dependency> 
    <groupId>software.amazon.dsql</groupId> 
    <artifactId>aurora-dsql-hibernate-dialect</artifactId> 
    <version>2.2.0</version>

The Aurora DSQL JDBC Connector handles IAM token generation automatically. AWS 
recommends it for connecting to Aurora DSQL from Java applications, including those 
using Hibernate.

Explicitly specify AuroraDSQLDialect. Otherwise, Hibernate selects PostgreSQLDialect from 
the database metadata. You can conﬁgure the dialect in several ways:
hibernate.properties
Add the following line to your hibernate.properties ﬁle:
hibernate.dialect=software.amazon.dsql.hibernate.dialect.AuroraDSQLDialect

Secure Sockets Layer (SSL) enabled.

negotiation (pgJDBC 42.7.4 or later) by default, so the connection uniform resource locator 
(URL) needs no SSL parameters.

and region with the values for your cluster.
hibernate.connection.url=jdbc:aws-dsql:postgresql://cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws/
postgres

Basic usage
The following example creates a Hibernate SessionFactory connected to Aurora DSQL and 
performs a basic read operation.
import org.hibernate.Session;
import org.hibernate.SessionFactory;
import org.hibernate.Transaction;
import org.hibernate.cfg.Configuration;
Configuration config = new Configuration() 
    .setProperty("hibernate.dialect", 
 "software.amazon.dsql.hibernate.dialect.AuroraDSQLDialect") 
    .setProperty("hibernate.connection.url", 
        "jdbc:aws-dsql:postgresql://cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws/postgres") 
    .setProperty("hibernate.connection.username", "admin") 
    .setProperty("hibernate.connection.driver_class", 
 "software.amazon.dsql.jdbc.DSQLConnector") 
    .addAnnotatedClass(MyEntity.class);
SessionFactory sessionFactory = config.buildSessionFactory();
try (Session session = sessionFactory.openSession()) { 
    Transaction tx = session.beginTransaction(); 
    try { 
        MyEntity result = session.get(MyEntity.class, entityId); 
        tx.commit(); 
    } catch (RuntimeException e) { 
        tx.rollback();  // roll back so the session can be reused or closed cleanly 
        throw e; 
    }
}

Using the Aurora DSQL dialect for Hibernate with connection pooling
The dialect works with any JDBC connection pool. The recommended pool for production use is
HikariCP, which is the default pool in Spring Boot.
Conﬁgure HikariCP with the DsqlExceptionOverride class so that the pool keeps healthy 
connections after Aurora DSQL rejects an unsupported statement, instead of evicting them.
import com.zaxxer.hikari.HikariDataSource;
import org.springframework.boot.jdbc.autoconfigure.DataSourceProperties;
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Bean;
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Configuration;
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Primary;
Hibernate dialect 338
=====PAGE 351=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
import org.springframework.boot.context.properties.ConfigurationProperties;
@Configuration(proxyBeanMethods = false)
public class DsqlDataSourceConfig { 
    @Bean 
    @Primary 
    public HikariDataSource dataSource(DataSourceProperties properties) { 
        HikariDataSource hds = properties.initializeDataSourceBuilder() 
            .type(HikariDataSource.class) 
            .build(); 
        hds.setMaxLifetime(3300000); // 55 minutes 
        hds.setExceptionOverrideClassName(DsqlExceptionOverride.class.getName()); 
        return hds; 
    }
}
Connection maxLifetime conﬁguration
Set the maxLifetime parameter to less than 3,600,000 milliseconds (one hour), which is 
the maximum connection duration that Aurora DSQL allows. Setting a lower maxLifetime
allows the connection pool to proactively recycle connections, which is more eﬃcient than 
handling connection timeout errors from the database.
Keeping healthy connections with DsqlExceptionOverride
Some Aurora DSQL errors leave the connection healthy, such as OCC conﬂicts (SQLSTATE 40001) 
and unsupported statements (SQLSTATE 0Axxx). By default, HikariCP evicts connections on 0A000. 
Implement SQLExceptionOverride and register it with HikariCP so that the pool keeps these 
connections:
import com.zaxxer.hikari.SQLExceptionOverride;
import java.sql.SQLException;
public class DsqlExceptionOverride implements SQLExceptionOverride { 
    @java.lang.Override 
    public Override adjudicate(SQLException sqlException) { 
        String sqlState = sqlException.getSQLState(); 
        if (sqlState == null) return Override.CONTINUE_EVICT; 
Hibernate dialect 339
=====PAGE 352=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
        // SQLSTATE 40001 = OCC conflict (OC000/OC001) — application retries the 
 transaction; connection is healthy 
        // SQLSTATE 0Axxx = feature_not_supported — Aurora DSQL rejected an unsupported 
 statement (for example, SAVEPOINT), 
        //   but the connection itself is healthy; evicting it would force an 
 unnecessary reconnect 
        if (sqlState.equals("40001") || sqlState.matches("0A\\d{3}")) { 
            return Override.DO_NOT_EVICT; 
        } 
        return Override.CONTINUE_EVICT; 
    }
}
Best practices
Primary key generation
UUID primary keys are the recommended choice in Aurora DSQL because they avoid sequence 
contention in a distributed environment. With @GeneratedValue, Hibernate generates 
the UUID in the application before the insert. The @ColumnDefault annotation adds a
gen_random_uuid() column default for rows that you insert outside Hibernate. The following 
Java code example adds a UUID primary key to a base entity class:
import jakarta.persistence.*;
import java.util.UUID;
import org.hibernate.annotations.ColumnDefault;
@MappedSuperclass
public class BaseEntity { 
    @Id 
    @GeneratedValue 
    @ColumnDefault("gen_random_uuid()") 
    @Column(name = "id", updatable = false, nullable = false) 
    private UUID id;
}
The dialect also supports sequence and identity-based integer primary keys. 
The dialect uses a default CACHE parameter of 65536 in sequence and identity 
column deﬁnitions. You can override this with the conﬁguration property
hibernate.dialect.aurora_dsql.sequence_cache_size.
Hibernate dialect 340
=====PAGE 353=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
@Entity
public class OrderEntity { 
    @Id 
    @GeneratedValue(strategy = GenerationType.SEQUENCE) 
    @SequenceGenerator(name = "order_seq", sequenceName = "order_seq", 
                       initialValue = 1000, allocationSize = 100) 
    private Integer id;
}
Auto-DDL
For production, use explicit DDL scripts instead of automatic schema generation 
(hbm2ddl.auto=create or update) to maintain full control over table deﬁnitions, index creation, 
and schema migrations.
Concurrency control and locking
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). Use only two Hibernate lock modes:
• LockModeType.NONE – Uses OCC instead of an explicit database lock. Aurora DSQL checks only 
the rows that the transaction writes. If a concurrent transaction modiﬁes one of those rows, the 
transaction that commits second fails with an OCC error (OC000, SQLSTATE 40001).
• LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE – Adds SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, which adds the 
selected rows to the commit-time conﬂict check instead of locking them. If another transaction 
changes those rows, the transaction that commits second fails with SQLSTATE 40001.
Use only NONE and PESSIMISTIC_WRITE, the two lock modes that the dialect overrides for Aurora 
DSQL compatibility. Hibernate core handles other lock modes and can generate clauses such as
SELECT ... FOR SHARE and SKIP LOCKED. For the locking clauses that Aurora DSQL supports, 
see SELECT command.
Handling OCC errors with transaction retry
Because OCC errors are retryable, you can retry the transaction. The following Java code example 
implements a retry aspect around your transactional methods:
import org.aspectj.lang.ProceedingJoinPoint;
import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Around;
Hibernate dialect 341
=====PAGE 354=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
import org.aspectj.lang.annotation.Aspect;
import org.springframework.core.Ordered;
import org.springframework.core.annotation.Order;
import org.springframework.dao.CannotAcquireLockException;
import org.springframework.orm.ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException;
import org.springframework.stereotype.Component;
import org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Propagation;
import org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional;
import java.util.concurrent.ThreadLocalRandom;
@Aspect
@Component
@Order(Ordered.HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE) // run outside the transaction so commit-time OCC 
 errors are retried
public class TransactionRetryAspect { 
    private static final int MAX_RETRIES = 3; 
    @Around("@annotation(org.springframework.transaction.annotation.Transactional)") 
    public Object retryOnOccConflict(ProceedingJoinPoint joinPoint) throws Throwable { 
        int attempt = 0; 
        while (true) { 
            try { 
                return joinPoint.proceed(); 
            } catch (ObjectOptimisticLockingFailureException | 
 CannotAcquireLockException e) { 
                if (++attempt >= MAX_RETRIES) throw e; 
                Thread.sleep(ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextLong(100L << attempt)); // 
 exponential backoff with jitter 
            } 
        } 
    }
}

a serialization error (OC000 / SQLSTATE 40001), which you

Authentication
The Aurora DSQL JDBC Connector automatically handles Aurora DSQL authentication by 
generating IAM tokens for each new connection. If you don't provide the AWS Region in the 
connection URL, the connector parses it from the cluster hostname.
For more information about authentication in Aurora DSQL, see Authentication and authorization.
Admin vs regular users
• Users named "admin" automatically use admin authentication tokens.
• All other users use non-admin authentication tokens.
• The JDBC connector generates tokens dynamically for each new connection.

Aurora DSQL adapter for Django
The Amazon Aurora DSQL adapter for Django (aurora-dsql-django) integrates Django object-
relational mapping (ORM) with Aurora DSQL. It handles IAM authentication, Secure Sockets Layer 
(SSL) conﬁguration, DDL generation diﬀerences (async indexes, identity/sequence columns), and 
connection management, so you can use the full Django ORM against a distributed Aurora DSQL 
cluster with minimal conﬁguration. For more information, see the Aurora DSQL adapter for Django
and the Django pet clinic example application on the GitHub website.
About the adapter
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a serverless, distributed relational database service optimized 
for transactional workloads. Aurora DSQL has speciﬁc requirements, including IAM-based 
authentication and asynchronous index creation. Aurora DSQL supports foreign key constraints 
enforced through optimistic concurrency control (OCC): foreign key (FK) conﬂicts raise a 
Django adapter 345
=====PAGE 358=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
serialization error that your application can retry. As of v0.7.0, the Django adapter enables
supports_foreign_keys = True and emits REFERENCES constraints in migrations and DDL.
With the Aurora DSQL adapter for Django, you can use the full Django ORM against Aurora 
DSQL without manually accounting for its behavioral diﬀerences. The adapter addresses these 
requirements by customizing the Django database backend.
What is Aurora DSQL authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens.
• Token generation: The connector generates authentication tokens from your AWS credentials. 
Tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes.
The Aurora DSQL adapter for Django automatically generates IAM authentication tokens when 
establishing connections, using the aurora-dsql-python-connector.
Features
The adapter provides the following features:
• Automatic IAM authentication – Generates IAM tokens automatically for each connection from 
your AWS credentials.
• SSL – Conﬁgure SSL through OPTIONS. The adapter supports the require and verify-full
modes.
• UUID primary keys – AutoField and BigAutoField map to universally unique identiﬁer 
(UUID) columns with a gen_random_uuid() default.
• Sequence and identity keys – Opt-in integer primary keys use GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS 
IDENTITY.
• Async index creation – Creates all indexes with CREATE INDEX ASYNC.
• Foreign key support – supports_foreign_keys = True, so migrations emit REFERENCES
constraints with OCC-based enforcement. For the foreign key options that Aurora DSQL 
supports, see Working with foreign key constraints in Aurora DSQL.
• OCC error handling – Surfaces OC000 (data conﬂict) and OC001 (schema conﬂict) so that your 
application can retry.
• Django compatibility – Works with Django 4.2 long-term support (LTS), 5.0, 5.1, and 5.2 LTS.
Django adapter 346
=====PAGE 359=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Prerequisites
Before you use the adapter, make sure that you have the following:
• Python 3.10 or higher
• Django 4.2 or higher
• psycopg (v3) or psycopg2
• AWS credentials with dsql:DbConnectAdmin permission (admin) or dsql:DbConnect (non-
admin)
Installation
Use Python 3.10 or newer. Install the adapter, the Python connector, and the recommended 
psycopg v3 driver in the same environment:
PROMPT> python3 -m pip install aurora-dsql-django
PROMPT> python3 -m pip install "aurora-dsql-python-connector[psycopg]>=0.2.7" 
 "psycopg[binary]"
If you use psycopg2 instead, install the connector and psycopg2 driver without the psycopg v3 
extra:
PROMPT> python3 -m pip install aurora-dsql-django
PROMPT> python3 -m pip install "aurora-dsql-python-connector>=0.2.7" psycopg2-binary

settings.py — minimal
In the following example, replace cluster-id and region with the values for your cluster, and
admin with your database user.
DATABASES = { 
    'default': { 
        'ENGINE': 'aurora_dsql_django', 
        'HOST':   'cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws', 
        'USER':   'admin', 
        'NAME':   'postgres',

Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
        'OPTIONS': { 
            'sslmode': 'verify-full', 
        } 
    }
}
With verify-full, the adapter defaults to sslrootcert="system", which requires libpq 16 
or later. On Linux, the psycopg[binary] and psycopg2-binary wheels also can't locate the 
system trust store, so set the SSL_CERT_FILE environment variable to your certiﬁcate authority 
(CA) bundle. Alternatively, pass the bundle path as sslrootcert, as shown in the next example. 
This works with any libpq version.
settings.py — with a custom certiﬁcate bundle
DATABASES = { 
    'default': { 
        'ENGINE': 'aurora_dsql_django', 
        'HOST':   'cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws', 
        'USER':   'admin', 
        'NAME':   'postgres', 
        'OPTIONS': { 
            'sslmode':    'verify-full', 
            'sslrootcert': '/path/to/cert.pem',  # omit to use the system trust store 
        } 
    }
}

Primary key strategies
UUID (default — recommended)
import uuid
from django.db import models
class Owner(models.Model): 
    id = models.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4, editable=False) 
    name = models.CharField(max_length=30) 
    city = models.CharField(max_length=80)
uuid.uuid4 generates the UUID client-side before the INSERT. When you keep the default
AutoField or BigAutoField (no explicit id ﬁeld), the adapter uses server-generated UUIDs 
(gen_random_uuid()).
Identity / sequence keys (opt-in)
Enable in settings.py:
Django adapter 349
=====PAGE 362=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
DATABASES = { 
    'default': { 
        'ENGINE': 'aurora_dsql_django', 
        # ... 
        'USE_SEQUENCE_AUTOFIELDS': True, 
        'SEQUENCE_CACHE_SIZE': 65536, 
    }
}
With this ﬂag set, AutoField and BigAutoField generate BIGINT GENERATED BY DEFAULT 
AS IDENTITY (CACHE 65536) columns instead of UUID.
SequenceAutoField (per-model explicit sequences)
from aurora_dsql_django import SequenceAutoField
class Owner(models.Model): 
    id = SequenceAutoField(primary_key=True) 
    name = models.CharField(max_length=30)
Deﬁning models
Foreign key ﬁelds
Aurora DSQL supports foreign key constraints with OCC-based enforcement — conﬂicts raise a 
serialization error (OC000 / SQLSTATE 40001) that your application can retry — and supports
NO ACTION, RESTRICT, CASCADE, SET NULL, SET DEFAULT, MATCH SIMPLE, MATCH FULL, and 
deferrable foreign keys.
Django generates REFERENCES constraints in migrations. Deﬁne FK ﬁelds normally —
db_constraint=True (the default):
class Pet(models.Model): 
    id    = models.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4, editable=False) 
    name  = models.CharField(max_length=30) 
    owner = models.ForeignKey(Owner, on_delete=models.CASCADE, null=True)
When adding FK constraints to existing tables, the adapter automatically issues ALTER 
TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID followed by ALTER TABLE ASYNC ... 
VALIDATE CONSTRAINT — you don't need to run these manually.
Django adapter 350
=====PAGE 363=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
For CHECK constraints added via ALTER TABLE, the adapter issues the NOT VALID and VALIDATE 
CONSTRAINT calls, but the migration doesn't wait for the async validation job. Check the 
corresponding job in sys.jobs promptly and conﬁrm that it succeeded. The constraint becomes 
valid only after the job succeeds.
Important
To add an FK column to an existing table, add the column with null=True, backﬁll 
all rows, and keep the column nullable. Adding the column as non-nullable raises
NotSupportedError. The following example shows the supported steps:
# Step 1: add as nullable — this succeeds
owner = models.ForeignKey(Owner, on_delete=models.CASCADE, null=True)
# Step 2: backfill owner_id for existing rows; keep the column nullable
Many-to-many relationships — use a custom intermediate table
Deﬁne the intermediate table explicitly rather than relying on Django's auto-generated one. An 
explicit through-model gives you full control over the primary key type, ensuring it's compatible 
with Aurora DSQL:
class VetSpecialties(models.Model): 
    id        = models.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4, editable=False) 
    vet       = models.ForeignKey('Vet', on_delete=models.CASCADE) 
    specialty = models.ForeignKey('Specialty', on_delete=models.CASCADE) 
    class Meta: 
        constraints = [ 
            models.UniqueConstraint(fields=['vet', 'specialty'], 
 name='unique_vet_specialty'), 
        ]
class Vet(models.Model): 
    id          = models.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4, 
 editable=False) 
    name        = models.CharField(max_length=30) 
    specialties = models.ManyToManyField('Specialty', through='VetSpecialties')
Django adapter 351
=====PAGE 364=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
One-to-one relationships
class Vet(models.Model): 
    id    = models.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4, editable=False) 
    name  = models.CharField(max_length=30) 
    owner = models.OneToOneField( 
        Owner, 
        on_delete=models.SET_DEFAULT, 
        null=True, blank=True, default=None 
    )
Migrations
PROMPT> python3 manage.py makemigrations pet_clinic
PROMPT> python3 manage.py migrate pet_clinic 0001
Important
Remove the default django.contrib.admin and django.contrib.auth apps from
INSTALLED_APPS if you aren't using them — they depend on integer auto-increment 
primary keys (PKs) and may fail with Aurora DSQL's UUID default.
OCC retry pattern
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). Conﬂicting concurrent transactions raise a
django.db.Error with one of two error codes — OC000 (data conﬂict: two transactions modiﬁed 
the same row) or OC001 (schema conﬂict: cached schema catalog is out of date). Wrap write views 
in a retry decorator:
import random
import time
from django.db import Error
OCC_SQLSTATE = "40001"
def _is_occ_error(exc): 
    # Django wraps the driver exception; the original lives in __cause__ 
    cause = getattr(exc, "__cause__", None) or getattr(exc, "__context__", None) 
    return ( 
Django adapter 352
=====PAGE 365=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
        getattr(cause, "sqlstate", None) == OCC_SQLSTATE   # psycopg (v3) 
        or getattr(cause, "pgcode", None) == OCC_SQLSTATE  # psycopg2 
    )
def with_retries(retries=3, initial_wait=1, delay_factor=2): 
    def decorator(view): 
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs): 
            delay = initial_wait 
            for attempt in range(retries): 
                try: 
                    return view(*args, **kwargs) 
                except Error as e: 
                    if not _is_occ_error(e): 
                        raise  # non-retryable — propagate immediately 
                    if attempt == retries - 1: 
                        raise  # exhausted retries 
                    print(f"OCC conflict, retrying (attempt {attempt+1}/{retries})") 
                    time.sleep(random.uniform(0, delay))  # exponential backoff with 
 jitter 
                    delay *= delay_factor 
        return wrapper 
    return decorator
Apply to view methods:
from django.db.transaction import atomic
@with_retries()
@atomic
def post(self, request, *args, **kwargs): 
    # ... write logic ...

INSTALLED_APPS = [ 
    'your_app',           # add your app 
    # remove 'django.contrib.admin', 'django.contrib.auth', and so on 
    # unless you have reconfigured them for UUID PKs
]

Aurora DSQL dialect for SQLAlchemy
The Amazon Aurora DSQL dialect for SQLAlchemy (aurora-dsql-sqlalchemy) integrates 
SQLAlchemy object-relational mapping (ORM) with Aurora DSQL. It handles IAM authentication, 
Secure Sockets Layer (SSL) conﬁguration, DDL generation diﬀerences (async indexes, identity 
columns), and connection management, so you can use the full SQLAlchemy API against a 
distributed Aurora DSQL cluster with minimal conﬁguration. For more information, see the Aurora 
DSQL dialect for SQLAlchemy and the SQLAlchemy pet clinic example application on the GitHub 
website.
This topic contains the following sections:
• Prerequisites
• Installation
• Quick start
• Conﬁguration
• Basic usage
• Primary key generation
• Index options
• OCC retry pattern
• SSL conﬁguration
• Connection pooling
• Best practices
• Dialect features reference
SQLAlchemy dialect 356
=====PAGE 369=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
About the dialect
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a serverless, distributed relational database service optimized 
for transactional workloads. Aurora DSQL has speciﬁc requirements, including IAM-based 
authentication, asynchronous index creation, DDL transaction restrictions, and foreign key 
enforcement through optimistic concurrency control (OCC), where conﬂicts raise a serialization 
error that your application can retry.
With the Aurora DSQL dialect for SQLAlchemy, you can use the full SQLAlchemy ORM API against 
Aurora DSQL without manually accounting for its behavioral diﬀerences. The dialect handles these 
diﬀerences automatically.
What is Aurora DSQL authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens.
• Token generation: The connector generates authentication tokens from your AWS credentials. 
Tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes.
The Aurora DSQL dialect for SQLAlchemy works with the aurora-dsql-python-connector, 
which automatically generates IAM authentication tokens when establishing connections.
Features
The dialect provides the following features:
• Automatic IAM authentication – Generates IAM tokens automatically from your AWS 
credentials.
• SSL – Defaults to verify-full mode with the system certiﬁcate authority (CA) trust store.
• Foreign key support – The dialect supports FOREIGN KEY constraints. Conﬂicts raise a 
serialization error (OC000 / SQLSTATE 40001), so your application needs retry logic. For the 
foreign key options that Aurora DSQL supports, see Working with foreign key constraints in 
Aurora DSQL.
• Async index creation – Creates all indexes with CREATE INDEX ASYNC.
• Identity columns and sequences – autoincrement=True generates GENERATED BY DEFAULT 
AS IDENTITY (CACHE 65536); Identity(always=True, cache=65536) generates
GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY (CACHE 65536).
SQLAlchemy dialect 357
=====PAGE 370=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• UUID primary keys – Supports server-generated universally unique identiﬁers (UUIDs) through
gen_random_uuid().
• Index extensions – Supports the INCLUDE clause and the NULLS NOT DISTINCT index option.
• Driver support – Compatible with psycopg (v3, default and recommended), psycopg2, and 
asyncpg (through the aurora-dsql-python-connector directly).
Prerequisites
Before you use the dialect, make sure that you have the following:
• Python 3.10 or higher
• SQLAlchemy 2.0.42 or higher
• One of the following PostgreSQL drivers:
• psycopg 3.2.0 or higher (psycopg[binary]) — default and recommended
• psycopg2 2.9.8 or higher (psycopg2-binary)
• AWS credentials with dsql:DbConnectAdmin permission (admin) or dsql:DbConnect (non-
admin)
Note
The aurora-dsql-sqlalchemy dialect supports psycopg and psycopg2. For asyncpg 
connections, use the aurora-dsql-python-connector directly.
Installation
PROMPT> pip install aurora-dsql-sqlalchemy
PROMPT> pip install "aurora-dsql-python-connector[psycopg]>=0.2.7"  # includes 
 psycopg_pool and is_occ_error
# Install a driver (choose one):
PROMPT> pip install "psycopg[binary]"   # psycopg v3 (recommended)
PROMPT> pip install psycopg2-binary     # psycopg2

Use pip install "psycopg[binary]". The psycopg-binary package is a prebuilt-
wheel distribution that isn't intended for direct installation.
Quick start
The following example creates an engine. In this example, replace cluster-id and region with 
the values for your cluster.
from aurora_dsql_sqlalchemy import create_dsql_engine
engine = create_dsql_engine( 
    host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", 
    user="admin", 
    driver="psycopg",   # or "psycopg2"
)
When you call create_dsql_engine, it automatically does the following:
• Generates a short-lived IAM auth token via aurora-dsql-python-connector
• Conﬁgures SSL (sslmode=verify-full, sslrootcert=system)
• Enables direct TLS negotiation when libpq >= 17 is available
• Sets up connection pooling with sensible defaults

from sqlalchemy import create_engine

                 host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", database="postgres")

Basic usage
The following example deﬁnes a model, creates its table, and then inserts and queries a row:
from sqlalchemy import String
from sqlalchemy.dialects.postgresql import UUID
from sqlalchemy.orm import DeclarativeBase, Mapped, Session, mapped_column
from sqlalchemy.sql import text
from aurora_dsql_sqlalchemy import create_dsql_engine
engine = create_dsql_engine(host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", user="admin")
class Base(DeclarativeBase): 
    pass
class Owner(Base): 
    __tablename__ = "owner" 
    id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(UUID, primary_key=True, 
 default=text("gen_random_uuid()")) 
    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(String(30)) 
    city: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(String(80))
# Create tables
Base.metadata.create_all(engine)
# Insert
with Session(engine) as session: 
    session.add(Owner(name="Mary Major", city="Seattle")) 
    session.commit()
SQLAlchemy dialect 361
=====PAGE 374=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
# Query
with Session(engine) as session: 
    owners = session.query(Owner).filter_by(city="Seattle").all() 
    print(owners)
Primary key generation
UUID (recommended)
Server-generated UUIDs are the recommended primary key type for Aurora DSQL.
from sqlalchemy.dialects.postgresql import UUID
from sqlalchemy.sql import text
id: Mapped[str] = mapped_column( 
    UUID, 
    primary_key=True, 
    default=text("gen_random_uuid()")
)
Sequences
from sqlalchemy import BigInteger, Sequence
id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column( 
    BigInteger, 
    primary_key=True, 
    default=Sequence("my_seq", cache=65536)
)
Aurora DSQL requires sequence cache to be either 1 or >= 65536. The dialect automatically adds
CACHE 65536 (the minimum allowed non-trivial value) if you don't specify a cache, and upgrades
INTEGER sequences to BIGINT.
Identity columns
from sqlalchemy import BigInteger, Identity
id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column( 
    BigInteger, 
    Identity(always=True, cache=65536),  # generates: GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY 
 (CACHE 65536) 
SQLAlchemy dialect 362
=====PAGE 375=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
    primary_key=True,
)
# Or using autoincrement (dialect adds CACHE 65536 automatically):
id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(BigInteger, primary_key=True, autoincrement=True)
Index options
INCLUDE clause
from sqlalchemy import Index
Index("idx_name", table.c.id, auroradsql_include=["name", "email"])
# # CREATE INDEX ASYNC idx_name ON table (id) INCLUDE (name, email)
NULLS NOT DISTINCT
Index("idx_unique_col", table.c.column, unique=True, 
 auroradsql_nulls_not_distinct=True)
# # CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ASYNC idx_unique_col ON table (column) NULLS NOT DISTINCT
OCC retry pattern
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). Conﬂicting concurrent transactions raise a
DBAPIError with one of two error codes:
• OC000 — Data conﬂict: two transactions modiﬁed the same row concurrently.
• OC001 — Schema conﬂict: the transaction's cached schema catalog is out of date.
Applications should catch both and retry. The following example retries a statement when an OCC 
conﬂict occurs:
import random
import time
from sqlalchemy import text
from sqlalchemy.exc import DBAPIError
from aurora_dsql_psycopg import is_occ_error  # requires aurora-dsql-python-connector 
 >=0.2.7
SQLAlchemy dialect 363
=====PAGE 376=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
def execute_with_retry(engine, statement, max_retries=5): 
    with engine.connect() as conn: 
        for attempt in range(max_retries): 
            try: 
                conn.execute(text(statement)) 
                conn.commit() 
                return 
            except DBAPIError as e: 
                conn.rollback() 
                if not is_occ_error(e.orig): 
                    raise 
                if attempt == max_retries - 1: 
                    raise 
                time.sleep(random.uniform(0, 0.1 * 2 ** attempt))  # exponential 
 backoff with jitter
SSL conﬁguration
Aurora DSQL requires TLS for all connections. The following table describes the supported
sslmode values.
sslmode value Behavior
verify-full  (default) Veriﬁes server certiﬁcate and hostname — recommended
verify-ca Veriﬁes certiﬁcate only, not hostname
require Encrypts connection but skips certiﬁcate veriﬁcation
sslrootcert="system" uses the operating system's CA trust store. On Linux, the
psycopg[binary] and psycopg2-binary wheels can't locate the system trust store. Set 
the SSL_CERT_FILE environment variable to your CA bundle, or pass the bundle path as
sslrootcert. To pin a speciﬁc CA:
engine = create_dsql_engine( 
    host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", 
    user="admin", 
    sslrootcert="/path/to/custom-ca.pem",
)
SQLAlchemy dialect 364
=====PAGE 377=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Important
The default sslrootcert="system" works only with sslmode="verify-full". 
To use verify-ca, set sslrootcert to a CA ﬁle path. To use require, pass
sslrootcert=None to clear the default:
engine = create_dsql_engine( 
    host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", 
    user="admin", 
    sslmode="require", 
    sslrootcert=None,
)
Connection pooling
create_dsql_engine creates a pooled engine by default.
engine = create_dsql_engine( 
    host="cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws", 
    user="admin", 
    pool_size=10,       # steady-state connections 
    max_overflow=20,    # burst capacity above pool_size
)
Important
Aurora DSQL enforces a one-hour maximum connection duration. The cluster closes 
long-lived connections. Set pool_recycle below 3600 to avoid race conditions — 3300 
provides a safe margin:
engine = create_dsql_engine(..., pool_recycle=3300)  # recycle before the 1-hour 
 limit
Best practices
• Use UUID primary keys with gen_random_uuid() to avoid sequence hot-spots in distributed 
writes.
SQLAlchemy dialect 365
=====PAGE 378=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• The dialect supports foreign key constraints, including referential actions, match types, and 
deferrable foreign keys. Enforcement is OCC-based: foreign key (FK) conﬂicts raise a serialization 
error (OC000 / SQLSTATE 40001) that you can retry in the same way as general write conﬂicts. 
FK constraint violations (referencing a non-existent row) raise a separate exception class and 
aren't retryable; don't use is_occ_error to gate retries on those.
• Aurora DSQL supports SET CONSTRAINTS for foreign keys only. For existing tables, add FK or 
CHECK constraints using ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID; the dialect 
appends NOT VALID automatically. Validating existing rows requires a separate async step. 
Record the job_id that ALTER TABLE ASYNC returns, then check the succeeded value that
sys.wait_for_job returns:
ALTER TABLE t ADD CONSTRAINT fk_name FOREIGN KEY (col) REFERENCES other(id) NOT 
 VALID;
ALTER TABLE ASYNC t VALIDATE CONSTRAINT fk_name;
-- Use the job_id returned by the preceding statement:
CALL sys.wait_for_job('returned_job_id');  -- verify succeeded is true
Conﬁrm that sys.wait_for_job returns succeeded as true. convalidated becomes true
only after the job succeeds.
• Wrap write transactions in OCC retry loops to handle concurrent conﬂicts.
• Set pool_recycle=3300 to stay within Aurora DSQL's one-hour connection duration limit with 
a safe margin.
• Use INCLUDE indexes for covering queries to reduce secondary reads.
• Use Identity(always=True, cache=65536) or an explicit Sequence with cache=65536
for integer primary keys. Without an explicit cache argument, Identity(always=True)
generates GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY without a CACHE clause. Aurora DSQL supports 
sequence cache values of 1 or at least 65536, so include an explicit supported cache value.
• Issue DDL and DML in separate transactions. Each transaction can contain only one DDL 
statement.
SQLAlchemy dialect 366
=====PAGE 379=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide

Aurora DSQL adapter for Tortoise ORM
Like the other adapters and dialects for Amazon Aurora DSQL, the Aurora DSQL adapter for 
Tortoise ORM (aurora-dsql-tortoise-orm) integrates an object-relational mapping (ORM) 
framework with Aurora DSQL. It provides custom backends for both asyncpg and psycopg
drivers, with Aurora DSQL-aware schema generators for asynchronous index creation, IDENTITY 
columns, and foreign key enforcement through optimistic concurrency control (OCC), and IAM 
authentication, so you can use the full Tortoise ORM API against a distributed Aurora DSQL cluster. 
For more information, see the Aurora DSQL adapter for Tortoise ORM and the Tortoise ORM pet 
clinic example application on the GitHub website.
About the adapter
Amazon Aurora DSQL is a serverless, distributed relational database service optimized 
for transactional workloads. Aurora DSQL has speciﬁc requirements, including IAM-based 
authentication, asynchronous index creation, universally unique identiﬁer (UUID) primary keys by 
default, and OCC-based foreign key enforcement. Foreign key (FK) conﬂicts raise a serialization 
error that your application can retry.
With the Aurora DSQL adapter for Tortoise ORM, you can use the full Tortoise ORM API against 
Aurora DSQL without manually accounting for its behavioral diﬀerences. The adapter addresses 
these requirements with custom Tortoise ORM backends and schema generators.
What is Aurora DSQL authentication?
In Aurora DSQL, authentication involves:
• IAM authentication: All connections use IAM-based authentication with time-limited tokens.
• Token generation: The connector generates authentication tokens from your AWS credentials. 
Tokens have conﬁgurable lifetimes.
Tortoise ORM adapter 368
=====PAGE 381=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
The adapter uses a password factory that calls aurora-dsql-python-connector for each 
connection to generate IAM tokens automatically. Connections use Secure Sockets Layer (SSL), 
but the connector doesn't verify the server certiﬁcate by default. To turn on veriﬁcation, see SSL 
conﬁguration.
Features
The adapter provides the following features:
• Automatic IAM authentication – Generates IAM tokens automatically from your AWS 
credentials.
• Driver support – Provides custom backends for both the asyncpg and psycopg drivers.
• Async index creation – Creates all indexes with CREATE INDEX ASYNC.
• Foreign key support – The adapter emits REFERENCES constraints in generated DDL, with OCC-
based enforcement. For the foreign key options that Aurora DSQL supports, see Working with 
foreign key constraints in Aurora DSQL.
• UUID primary keys – UUIDField with uuid.uuid4 is the recommended primary key strategy.
• BigInt identity columns – BigIntField primary keys use GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS 
IDENTITY with CACHE 65536.
• aerich migrations – Works with aerich migration tooling.
• Connection uniform resource locator (URL) support – Supports the dsql+asyncpg:// and
dsql+psycopg:// URL schemes.
• OCC error handling – Surfaces OC000 (data conﬂict) and OC001 (schema conﬂict) so that your 
application can retry.
Prerequisites
Before you use the adapter, make sure that you have the following:
• Python 3.10 or higher
• AWS credentials with dsql:DbConnectAdmin permission (admin) or dsql:DbConnect (non-
admin)
• Your cluster endpoint and user — the code examples below read these from
CLUSTER_ENDPOINT and CLUSTER_USER environment variables, but you can supply them any 
way you prefer

=====PAGE 382=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
Installation
PROMPT> pip install "aurora-dsql-python-connector>=0.2.7"  # provides is_occ_error for 
 the retry example
# Install the adapter with one or both drivers:
PROMPT> pip install "aurora-dsql-tortoise-orm[asyncpg]"  # asyncpg backend
PROMPT> pip install "aurora-dsql-tortoise-orm[psycopg]"  # psycopg backend
Conﬁguration
Tortoise.init() — programmatic
import os
from tortoise import Tortoise
CLUSTER_ENDPOINT = os.environ["CLUSTER_ENDPOINT"]
CLUSTER_USER = os.environ.get("CLUSTER_USER", "admin")

    config={ 
        "connections": { 
            "default": { 
                "engine": "aurora_dsql_tortoise.asyncpg",   # or .psycopg 
                "credentials": { 
                    "host": CLUSTER_ENDPOINT, 
                    "user": CLUSTER_USER, 
                }, 
            } 
        },

                "default_connection": "default", 
            }

)

SSL conﬁguration
Aurora DSQL requires TLS for all connections. To verify the server certiﬁcate, set sslmode
to verify-full and sslrootcert to a certiﬁcate authority (CA) certiﬁcate ﬁle, such as 
Amazon Root CA 1. For more information, see Conﬁguring SSL/TLS certiﬁcates for Aurora 
DSQL connections. The asyncpg backend takes these keys in credentials and doesn't accept
sslrootcert="system". The psycopg backend takes them in server_settings:
# asyncpg
"credentials": { 
    "host": CLUSTER_ENDPOINT, 
    "user": CLUSTER_USER, 
    "sslmode": "verify-full", 
    "sslrootcert": "/path/to/custom-ca.pem",
},
# psycopg
"credentials": { 
    "host": CLUSTER_ENDPOINT, 
    "user": CLUSTER_USER, 
    "server_settings": {"sslmode": "verify-full", "sslrootcert": "/path/to/custom-
ca.pem"},
},
URL format
Before use, call register_asyncpg() to register the dsql+asyncpg:// URL scheme, or
register_psycopg() to register dsql+psycopg://. register_backends() registers both 
and requires both drivers. In the following example, replace cluster-id and region with the 
values for your cluster:
from aurora_dsql_tortoise import register_asyncpg
register_asyncpg()
await Tortoise.init( 
    db_url="dsql+asyncpg://admin@cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws/postgres", 
    modules={"models": ["myapp.models"]},
)
# or, after register_psycopg():
db_url = "dsql+psycopg://admin@cluster-id.dsql.region.on.aws/postgres"
Tortoise ORM adapter 371
=====PAGE 384=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
The adapter type-casts URL query parameters automatically (for example, ?
max_size=10&timeout=30).
Connection duration
Aurora DSQL enforces a one-hour maximum connection duration. The cluster closes long-lived 
connections.
asyncpg: max_inactive_connection_lifetime controls how long an idle connection stays 
in the pool before the pool closes it. It applies only to idle connections, so a continuously busy 
connection can still reach the one-hour limit. For workloads with long-running or continuously-
used connections, recycle connections in application logic. Alternatively, switch to the psycopg
backend. Its pool supports a max_lifetime parameter that caps total connection age regardless 
of activity.
psycopg backend
psycopg_pool defaults max_lifetime to 3600.0 seconds — exactly Aurora DSQL's hard 
limit. Set it explicitly to a lower value (for example, 3300) to give the pool time to recycle 
connections before the cluster closes them:
"credentials": { 
    "host": CLUSTER_ENDPOINT, 
    "user": CLUSTER_USER, 
    "max_lifetime": 3300,  # recycle before the 1-hour hard limit
}
Schema per user
For non-admin users, set CLUSTER_USER to the non-admin user name. The adapter uses
dsql:DbConnect instead of dsql:DbConnectAdmin for token generation automatically.
Deﬁning models
UUID primary key (recommended)
import uuid
from tortoise import fields
from tortoise.models import Model
Tortoise ORM adapter 372
=====PAGE 385=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
class Owner(Model): 
    id   = fields.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4) 
    name = fields.CharField(max_length=30) 
    city = fields.CharField(max_length=80) 
    class Meta: 
        table = "owner"
BigInt identity column (opt-in)
from tortoise import fields
from tortoise.models import Model
class Owner(Model): 
    id   = fields.BigIntField(primary_key=True)   # # GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY 
 (CACHE 65536) 
    name = fields.CharField(max_length=30) 
    class Meta: 
        table = "owner"
Important
Use UUIDField or BigIntField as primary keys. IntField and SmallIntField
primary keys raise a ValueError at schema generation time.
Foreign key relations
Aurora DSQL supports NO ACTION, RESTRICT, CASCADE, SET NULL, SET DEFAULT, MATCH 
SIMPLE, MATCH FULL, and deferrable foreign keys, and supports SET CONSTRAINTS for 
foreign keys only. Enforcement is OCC-based: FK conﬂicts raise a serialization error (OC000 /
SQLSTATE 40001), which you can detect with is_occ_error and retry. FK constraint violations 
(referencing a non-existent row) raise a distinct exception and aren't retryable. All DML on 
referenced or referencing tables incurs extra reads, and constraints added to existing tables use
ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID followed by ALTER TABLE ASYNC ... 
VALIDATE CONSTRAINT; the adapter's aerich integration handles this automatically.
class Pet(Model): 
    id     = fields.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4) 
Tortoise ORM adapter 373
=====PAGE 386=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
    name   = fields.CharField(max_length=30) 
    owner  = fields.ForeignKeyField("models.Owner", related_name="pets") 
    class Meta: 
        table = "pet"
The generated DDL includes an owner_id column with a REFERENCES owner(id) constraint. 
Enforcement is OCC-based — FK conﬂicts raise OC000, which your application can retry.
Many-to-many
class Vet(Model): 
    id          = fields.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4) 
    name        = fields.CharField(max_length=30) 
    specialties = fields.ManyToManyField("models.Specialty", related_name="vets") 
    class Meta: 
        table = "vet"
class Specialty(Model): 
    id   = fields.UUIDField(primary_key=True, default=uuid.uuid4) 
    name = fields.CharField(max_length=80) 
    class Meta: 
        table = "specialty"
Schema generation
generate_schemas() splits the DDL on ; (respecting quoted strings, dollar-quoted blocks, and 
comments) and executes each statement individually:
await Tortoise.generate_schemas()
This is safe to call — the splitter is quote/comment/dollar-quote aware.
Basic usage
from tortoise import Tortoise
import uuid
# Create
owner = await Owner.create(name="John Doe", city="Seattle")
Tortoise ORM adapter 374
=====PAGE 387=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
pet   = await Pet.create(name="Buddy", owner=owner)
# Query
found = await Pet.get(id=pet.id).prefetch_related("owner")
print(found.owner.name)
# Filter
owners = await Owner.filter(city="Seattle").all()
# Update
await Owner.filter(id=owner.id).update(city="Portland")
# Delete
await pet.delete()
await owner.delete()
# Close
await Tortoise.close_connections()
OCC retry pattern
Aurora DSQL uses optimistic concurrency control (OCC). Conﬂicting concurrent transactions raise a 
serialization failure with SQLSTATE 40001. The error message contains OC000 (data conﬂict: two 
transactions modiﬁed the same row) or OC001 (schema conﬂict: the transaction's cached schema 
catalog has become out of date). Wrap write operations in a retry loop:
import asyncio
import random
from aurora_dsql_asyncpg import is_occ_error  # requires aurora-dsql-python-connector 
 >=0.2.7
# psycopg backend: from aurora_dsql_psycopg import is_occ_error
async def with_retry(coro_fn, max_retries=5, base_delay=0.1): 
    for attempt in range(max_retries): 
        try: 
            return await coro_fn() 
        except Exception as e: 
            if not is_occ_error(e): 
                raise 
            if attempt == max_retries - 1: 
                raise 
            await asyncio.sleep(random.uniform(0, base_delay * 2 ** attempt))  # 
 exponential backoff with jitter
Tortoise ORM adapter 375
=====PAGE 388=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
# Usage
result = await with_retry(lambda: Owner.create(name="Jane", city="Seattle"))
aerich migrations
aerich works with the Aurora DSQL backends. Install aerich, and then run aerich init to point 
aerich at an Aurora DSQL backend:
PROMPT> pip install "aerich>=0.9.2"
PROMPT> aerich init -t myapp.config.TORTOISE_ORM
# myapp/config.py
TORTOISE_ORM = { 
    "connections": { 
        "default": { 
            "engine": "aurora_dsql_tortoise.asyncpg", 
            "credentials": {"host": CLUSTER_ENDPOINT, "user": CLUSTER_USER}, 
        } 
    }, 
    "apps": { 
        "models": { 
            "models": ["myapp.models", "aerich.models", "aurora_dsql_tortoise.aerich"], 
            "default_connection": "default", 
        } 
    },
}
Include the aurora_dsql_tortoise.aerich module in the models list so that migrations 
succeed on Aurora DSQL. It applies the Aurora DSQL-speciﬁc patches to aerich: UUID primary key 
for the Aerich table, RUN_IN_TRANSACTION=False, and single-DDL statement splitting.
SQL splitting
The adapter includes a split_sql(sql: str) -> list[str] utility. It correctly handles the 
following SQL elements when it splits statements:
• Single-quoted strings (including escaped '' quotes)
• Double-quoted identiﬁers
• Dollar-quoted strings ($$...$$ and $tag$...$tag$)
Tortoise ORM adapter 376
=====PAGE 389=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• Single-line comments (-- ...)
• Multi-line comments (/* ... */)
• Trailing/consecutive semicolons
The following Python code example splits a multi-statement SQL script into individual statements:
from aurora_dsql_tortoise.common.config import split_sql
statements = split_sql(""" 
    CREATE TABLE owner (id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()); 
    CREATE TABLE pet   (id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), owner_id UUID); 
    CREATE INDEX ASYNC idx_pet_owner ON pet (owner_id);
""")
# # ['CREATE TABLE owner ...', 'CREATE TABLE pet ...', 'CREATE INDEX ASYNC ...']

Accessing Aurora DSQL with PostgreSQL-compatible clients
Aurora DSQL uses the PostgreSQL wire protocol. You can connect to PostgreSQL using a variety 
of tools and clients, such as AWS CloudShell, psql, DBeaver, and DataGrip. The following table 
summarizes how Aurora DSQL maps common PostgreSQL connection parameters:

When connecting, Aurora DSQL requires a signed IAM authentication token in place of a traditional 
password. These temporary tokens are generated using AWS Signature Version 4 and are used only 
during connection establishment. Once connected, the session remains active until it ends or the 
client disconnects.
If you attempt to open a new session with an expired token, the connection request fails and a 
new token must be generated. For more information, see Generating an authentication token in 
Amazon Aurora DSQL.

Supported session parameters
Aurora DSQL automatically optimizes storage, connection management, and transaction 
processing, so it supports a focused set of session parameters. Aurora DSQL accepts the parameters 
listed in the following table. Parameters outside this set return the following error:
ERROR: setting configuration parameter "parameter_name" not supported
Aurora DSQL supports the following PostgreSQL session parameters. Unless otherwise noted, 
you can set these using SET, SET SESSION, or as connection string options. You can also use SET 
LOCAL to set any of these parameters for the duration of a single transaction. Use RESET to restore 
a parameter to its default value.
Supported session parameters

connections in monitoring.

database uses UTF-8 internally.

formatting

values (for example, ISO, MDY).

formatting
Sets the number of digits displayed for 
ﬂoating-point values. Some drivers, such as 
JDBC, use this parameter.

formatting
Sets the display format for interval values.
timezone Localization Sets the time zone for the session. Aurora 
DSQL stores all timezone-aware dates and 
times internally in UTC. This parameter 
controls how Aurora DSQL displays values to 
the client.

Aurora DSQL automatically manages the following aspects of your database, so you don't need to 
conﬁgure the corresponding PostgreSQL session parameters:
Session parameters 381
=====PAGE 394=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
• Connection and network parameters – Aurora DSQL manages connection lifecycle and TCP 
settings internally, including tcp_keepalives_idle, tcp_keepalives_interval, and
tcp_keepalives_count.
• Memory and resource parameters – Aurora DSQL automatically manages memory allocation, 
including work_mem and shared_buffers.
• Timeout parameters – Aurora DSQL enforces its own transaction duration limits, including
statement_timeout, lock_timeout, and idle_in_transaction_session_timeout.
• Replication and WAL parameters – Aurora DSQL handles replication automatically through its 
built-in multi-Region architecture, including wal_level and synchronous_commit.
• Logging parameters – Aurora DSQL manages logging internally, including log_statement and
client_min_messages.
• Transaction parameters – Aurora DSQL uses a ﬁxed REPEATABLE READ isolation level for all 
transactions. If your driver requires specifying an isolation level, use BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL 
REPEATABLE READ.
Access Aurora DSQL using SQL clients
Aurora DSQL supports multiple PostgreSQL-compatible clients for connecting to your cluster. The 
following sections describe how to connect using PostgreSQL with AWS CloudShell or your local 
command line, as well as GUI-based tools like DBeaver and JetBrains DataGrip. Each client requires 
a valid authentication token as described in the previous section.

• Use DBeaver to access Aurora DSQL

• Use Aurora DSQL driver for SQLTools

Use DBeaver to access Aurora DSQL
DBeaver is a universal SQL client that can be used to manage any database that has a JDBC driver. 
It is widely used among developers and database administrators because of its robust data viewing, 
SQL clients 382
=====PAGE 395=====
Amazon Aurora DSQL User Guide
editing, and management capabilities. Using DBeaver's cloud connectivity options, you can connect 
DBeaver to Aurora DSQL natively.
DBeaver Pro
DBeaver PRO products oﬀer native integration with Aurora DSQL as of version 25.3. Follow the 
instructions from DBeaver Documentation to connect to your Aurora DSQL cluster.
DBeaver Community Edition
DBeaver Community Edition is the free and open-source version. Visit the download page for 
installation instructions. In order to connect to DSQL from DBeaver Community Edition, you need 
to install the Aurora DSQL Plugin for DBeaver.
The Aurora DSQL Plugin for DBeaver is built on top of the Aurora DSQL Connector for JDBC and 
enables IAM authentication to Aurora DSQL clusters. It is conveniently installed through DBeaver 
UI and eliminates the need to write token generation code or manually supply a valid IAM token, 
simplifying the authentication while eliminating security risks associated with traditional user-
generated passwords.
Features
• IAM Authentication Support: Connect to Aurora DSQL clusters using AWS IAM credentials for 
secure, password-free authentication
• Automatic Driver Management: Seamlessly installs and conﬁgures the Aurora DSQL Connector 
for JDBC
• Flexible Connection Options: Choose between Host-based or JDBC URL-based connection 
conﬁguration
Aurora DSQL Plugin for DBeaver Installation
1. With DBeaver opened, Go to the Drop down menu Help → Install New Software
2. Click Add to add a new repository
3. Enter:
• Name: Aurora DSQL Plugin
• Location: https://awslabs.github.io/aurora-dsql-dbeaver-plugin/update-
site/
4. Check Aurora DSQL Connector for JDBC
